3	Survey date: 2026-09-26. Read-only. Primary corpus: the DB dump in `db/` (PT101 to PT159). The local markdown in `lsatu-wt-parser/Explanations/` (PT139 to PT159) was used to check layout and choice tags, which the dump does not include.

5	## 1. What the corpus actually is
6	
7	**Styles.** `lr_explanations.style` has four values: `single` 3,183, `standard` 236, `brief` 236, `another_way` 236. Every question with a `standard` row also has `brief` and `another_way`, and no question has both `standard` and `single`. So "the Standard explanation" is `standard` where it exists and `single` everywhere else. Both have the same shape (Argument breakdown steps, then five choice notes). 352 of 3,771 questions have no explanation at all (`is_explained = false`).

43	## 2. Quality by test range
44	
45	| PTs | Explained | Avg words | Complete | Em-dashes per 1k words | Notes |
46	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
47	| 101-122 | 1,158 | 553 | 85% | 0.5 | Full LSAT Lab voice. Many golden picks come from here. |
48	| 123-138 | 1,025 | 362 | 51% | 0.7 | Noticeably thinner. Most "incomplete" rows have a breakdown but no Goal step (346), and choice notes are often one or two lines. Some notes read like generic summaries (PT126-S4-Q20: "This introduces arbitrators, which are outside the scope"). |
49	| 139-156 | 1,097 | 588 | 91% | 0.6 | Same voice as 101-122. Consistently strong. |
50	| 157-159 | 139 | 672 | 89% | 7.1 | PT157/158 are explicit Standard. Breakdowns are in the house voice; choice notes are a different register (below). |
51	
52	**The PT157/158 explicit-Standard register.** Choice notes run about 100+ words each, open with formulas ("This answer captures...", "This answer identifies the argument's central flaw"), use em-dashes at roughly ten times the rate of everything else, and repeat the same point several ways. More importantly, they contain accuracy slips I did not find in the single-style text at the same rate:
53	- PT158-S3-Q2: the D note describes a different answer ("can effectively combine knowledge") from the actual choice ("can effectively solve many problems"); A is tagged "No Impact" while its note argues it undermines the argument.
54	- PT158-S2-Q16: the correct-answer note imports outside history (Plato's family wealth, Philip of Macedon) to justify the answer.
55	- PT158-S2-Q18: the A note argues against a claim the choice does not make.
56	
57	That is why no explicit-Standard item made the golden set, and why the house voice rules (no em-dashes) matter for the writing guide.
58	
59	**Two voices inside the single style.** Most text is one conversational author (voice A: "Hey, author", "Let's look for", FABS, Curious Fact, Given that / How can it be that). A second, more formal author appears in about 15% of questions (voice B: "Beware of answers that...", "If you chose this answer...", "This answer choice..."). Voice B is accurate and structured but lighter on prediction and analogy. Examples: PT146-S2-Q8, PT151-S2-Q1, PT152-S4-Q5, PT154-S1-Q10, PT155-S4-Q1. Both voices can produce a strong explanation; voice A produces most of the best ones.
60	
61	## 3. What separates strong from loose (the empirical comparison)
62	
63	I picked ten explanations that were clearly strong and ten that were complete but loose, read all twenty side by side, then measured a few things.
64	
65	- **Strong (10):** PT139-S4-Q18 Flaw, PT142-S4-Q22 Flaw, PT145-S2-Q19 Paradox, PT154-S1-Q18 NA, PT153-S2-Q13 Weaken, PT146-S2-Q13 Strengthen, PT147-S1-Q17 Evaluate, PT152-S2-Q21 Principle-Conform, PT150-S3-Q15 Agree/Disagree, PT144-S3-Q17 Role.
66	- **Loose but complete (10), with the failure each shows:** PT127-S2-Q3 Weaken (two wrong answers get one line each: "This supports the argument's explanation"); PT126-S4-Q20 Weaken (generic summary notes, a Goal that restates the task); PT124-S3-Q10 Role (short, and "Haha. This answer is unthinkably weird"); PT151-S2-Q18 Strengthen (117 words total; the 29% trap gets one sentence); PT146-S2-Q8 Flaw (every wrong answer is "The argument never discusses X", correct note is one line); PT142-S4-Q5 Principle (mechanical, no reason why the trap tempts); PT155-S2-Q10 Paradox (fine idea, thin notes); PT158-S3-Q2 Strengthen (long, but misquotes a choice); PT158-S2-Q16 Paradox (long, but brings in outside facts); PT148-S1-Q20 Method (sound analysis, but calls the correct answer "so, so dumb").
67	
68	Measured averages (strong vs loose):
69	
70	| Measure | Strong | Loose |
71	|---|---|---|
72	| Total words | 773 | 394 |
73	| Words in the Evaluate step | 169 | 77 |
74	| Words in the Goal step | 66 | 43 |
75	| Goal names 3+ content words from the stimulus | 0.9 | 0.5 |
76	| Avg words per wrong-answer note | 87 | 45 |
77	| Wrong-answer notes under 20 words (verdict only) | 0.0 per question | 1.1 per question |
78	| Wrong-answer notes that run an explicit test (negate it, does it weaken, does it trigger the rule, why should we believe it) | 1.4 of 4 | 0.2 of 4 |
79	| Words in the correct-answer note | 143 | 60 |
80	| Examples, analogies or worked numbers | 2.0 | 0.5 |
81	| Stated generalizations ("whenever", "on X questions", "usually") | 1.6 | 0.3 |
82	
83	What actually separates them, in order of how reliably it predicted my judgment:
84	
85	1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument.** Strong ones do real work before the choices: they build the objection ("Given that X, how could we argue not-Y?"), do the arithmetic (25% of $400 vs 13% of $1,000), or set the two populations side by side. Loose ones restate the stimulus and move on.
86	2. **The Goal names the answer, not the task.** Strong: "look for an answer saying the fish in the leafless trees are exposed to predators that hunt by sight." Loose: "Find the flaw that mistakes a response to danger for a cause", "Identify an answer choice that supports fairness." Strong Goals often give two acceptable shapes (a linking answer or a defender).
87	3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason tied to this question, and usually a test.** The loose group's signature is the verdict-only note: "This is irrelevant.", "That is unnecessary.", "The argument never discusses profits." Strong notes say what the answer claims, what was needed, and where they diverge, often by running the family's test on it (negate it; would it weaken; is the trigger established; does the conclusion match the rule's right side).
88	4. **The popular trap gets the most attention.** Strong explanations spend their longest wrong-answer note on the choice that drew 15-30% of students and say why it tempts. Loose ones treat it like every other wrong answer.
89	5. **The correct-answer note explains the fit, and often generalizes it.** Strong: "Another correct answer performing the same function could have said..." (PT146-S2-Q13); the karaoke/Jenga reason why A → C still works when you predicted A and B → C (PT139-S4-Q21). Loose: "This is the flaw."
90	6. **Fidelity.** Two loose items are long. Length did not save them: a misquoted answer choice, imported outside facts, or the author disowning the question destroys model value regardless of word count.
91	
92	What did not separate them: word count by itself (PT152-S4-Q19 is 479 words and strong; PT158-S2-Q16 is 716 and loose), the choice-tag vocabulary, and the presence of jokes (several strong ones joke; it only hurts when the joke is at the question's or the answer's expense).
93	
94	## 4. Rubric used for the shortlist
95	
96	Gate (hard requirement): full Argument breakdown and all five choice notes. Every shortlisted candidate passed.
97	
98	Scored 0 / 1 / 2:
99	
100	| Criterion | 2 | 1 | 0 |
101	|---|---|---|---|
102	| **P** Prediction | Goal names the content or shape of the right answer before the choices (may give two acceptable shapes) | Goal names the task or admits it cannot predict | No prediction |
103	| **X** Precision | Names the exact gap, flaw or relationship in this argument's words | Uses the right label but stays generic | Label only, or wrong |
104	| **E** Eliminations | Every wrong answer gets a question-grounded reason; the popular trap gets the most attention | Most do; one or two verdict-only notes | Verdict-only notes |
105	| **T** Transfer | States the move in a form that works on the next question of this type (the test, the rule, the recurring trap) | Transfer is implicit | None |
106	| **C** Clarity | Plain, no padding, no pasted repetition | Some padding or pasted notes | Hard to follow |
107	| **L** Learner value | Teaches what a student needs to understand and repeat the move: why the trap tempts, what to do when stuck, a worked example or analogy, a second correct answer that would also work | Justifies the key well but does not teach the move | Only justifies the key |
108	
109	Plus a **fidelity flag** (not scored; disqualifying if serious): `slip` factual or typo error, `tone` the author mocks the question or answer, `register` explicit-Standard PT157/158 prose, `transcription` damaged text.
110	
111	Learner value is the criterion that most often separated a 10 from a 12. Many explanations justify the key correctly (1) but only the best ones also show the student the move they will reuse (2).
112	
113	## 5. Step structure per family (DB, most common sequences)
114	
115	Labels normalized (parenthetical indicator words dropped: "Conclusion (therefore)" counts as Conclusion).
116	
117	- **Flaw (524):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (67%); Evidence > Conclusion > Evaluate (9%); with an Intermediate Conclusion step (5%). Evaluate usually names the famous flaw or builds the objection; Goal often gives both the formal and the "fails to consider" phrasing.
118	- **Must Be True / Most Supported (462):** Statements > Evaluate > Goal (45%); Statements only (16%). Statements is often a diagram. Evaluate scans for conditional, causal, math and pivot wording and names the inference to make.
119	- **Necessary Assumption (352):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (66%). Goal typically offers a linking answer and a defender answer, and says "negate it".
120	- **Weaken (335) / Strengthen (277):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (59% / 55%). Evaluate uses "Given that ... how could we argue that ..." or Curious Fact / Alternate Explanation / Plausibility.
121	- **Paradox (213):** "Apparent Paradox > Evaluate" (22%, older tests) or "Given that ... > How can it be that / Why is it that ... > Evaluate > Goal" (about 30% combined, often with Evaluate / Goal merged).
122	- **Sufficient Assumption (193):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (53%); Evidence > Conclusion > Evaluate (21%). Goal usually diagrams the missing link as the logic path.
123	- **Principle-Strengthen (181):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (48%). Goal is "If evidence, then conclusion" in general terms.
124	- **Principle-Conform (99):** mixed. Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (24%) when the principle is in the answers; Principle > Evaluate > Goal (9%) or Principle > Goal (7%) when it is in the stimulus.
125	- **Main Conclusion (144) / Role (126):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (42% / 53%), with an Intermediate Conclusion step in about 8%. Evaluate almost always opens with the reminder that the conclusion usually comes early (first sentence or after a pivot).
126	- **Parallel (100) / Parallel Flaw (123):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (49% / 67%), Evidence first in about 19-35%. Goal is an algebraic recipe (P1, P2, C) or a plain-language template.
127	- **Agree/Disagree (119):** speaker-named steps. "Speaker's Claims > Speaker's Responses > Goal", often a numbered claim list with "Does [speaker 2] disagree?" per claim.
128	- **Method (91):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (37%) for one speaker; "X's Conclusion > X's Evidence > Y's Response > Evaluate > Goal" for two speakers.
129	- **Evaluate (28):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (75%).
130	
131	## 6. Recurring phrasing conventions
132	
133	- **Frames:** "Given that ... how could we (still) argue that ..." (Weaken, Strengthen, NA, Flaw); "Curious Fact ... Author's Explanation ... Alternate Explanation / Plausibility" (any causal argument); "Given that ... How can it be that ..." (Paradox); "If Evidence, then Conclusion" (Principle, SA).
134	- **Tests in choice notes:** negate it and ask if it weakens (NA); "fails to consider X" means ask whether X weakens, "presumes X" means ask whether X is necessary (Flaw); "argues X by Y: X must match the conclusion and Y the evidence" (Method, Flaw); a rule can only prove its right-hand side (Principle, SA); "can we support the Agree position and the Disagree position?" (Agree/Disagree); "Why should we believe this?" (Role, Main Conclusion).
135	- **Recurring trap names:** Too Strong (the most common NA and MBT tag), Out of Scope, No Impact, Unclear Impact, No Distinction (Paradox), Deepens Paradox, Wrong Flaw / Not an Objection (Flaw), Bad Conclusion / Evidence / Trigger Match (Principle, Parallel), Premise / Last-Claim Trap / Opposing Position (Main Conclusion), Unsupported Agree/Disagree Position (Agree/Disagree), Fake Opposite / Illegal Negation (NA), Reversal (SA). Tags come from the local markdown; the dump does not include them.
136	- **Rules stated as rules:** "Most X can only be concluded from a Most X premise"; "some / sometimes / can is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, Paradox, SA"; "the second speaker almost never denies the first speaker's evidence"; "about 85-90% of Main Conclusion questions put the conclusion in the first sentence or after a pivot"; "on EXCEPT, three of the working answers usually work one way and one works another".
137	- **Voice habits that should not be copied:** mocking the question or answer ("hot garbage", "so, so dumb", "unthinkably weird", "dumpster fire"), and the PT157/158 em-dash register.
138	
139	## 7. `Explanations/targeted-questions/`
140	
141	Three files: a README and two explanations, PT134-S1-Q16 (Flaw, belief vs implication, answer A) and PT158-S4-Q24 (Weaken, food-supply chain, answer D). The README says they were "added for LSAT U use when Joshua needs specific questions available outside a full PrepTest-section explanation file."
142	
143	They are not LSAT Lab transcriptions. The layout is different and the parser will not read it: an H1 title, a metadata block (type, answer, LSAT U order, bank file), H2 sections for Stimulus, Prompt, Argument breakdown, **Written explanation** (a free prose section the LSAT Lab files do not have), Answer choice explanations with an italic tag, and a closing **Student takeaway**. The prose is written in LSAT U's own voice: no em-dashes, short sentences, jokes built on the stimulus ("the drug sneaks into the food supply wearing a tiny trench coat", "Still a raccoon in the kitchen"), and a one-line takeaway ("Break the link that matters. That's the move.").
144	
145	Authorship: git attributes them to `vnjkoxpl-42905` (Joshua's account) inside a bulk commit dated 2026-09-05 titled "Update RRC launch page copy", with no co-author line and no other reference in the repo or wiki. The style, the house-voice compliance and the README wording point to an AI assistant (Claude or Codex) drafting them for Joshua. That is an inference; the git history cannot confirm who typed them.
146	
147	They are useful as a picture of the target voice (especially "Written explanation" and "Student takeaway", which have no LSAT Lab equivalent). They are not in the golden set because they are outside the corpus and use a different structure.
148	
149	## 8. Caveats
150	
151	- All scores are one reader's judgment, formed by reading each candidate once. Treat 11 vs 12 as a tie.
152	- Features and difficulty come from the question bank. 110 explained questions have no bank match, and some shortlisted items have no features in the bank at all (PT143-S1-Q6, PT154-S1-Q10), so the coverage grid undercounts them.
153	- I read about 125 explanations in full or near-full, weighted toward the highest-scoring candidates in each family and difficulty band. I did not read all 3,000, so a better exemplar for a thin cell may exist.

17	## What separates a top-tier explanation from a loose one
18	
19	Length does not. These do:
20	
21	1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument** ("How could we argue the opposite?") instead of restating it.
22	2. **The Goal names the answer** it expects, not just the task.
23	3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason and usually a test.** Loose ones average one verdict-only note per question ("This is irrelevant").
24	4. **The most popular trap gets the most attention.**
25	5. **The correct-answer note teaches the move**, so a student can repeat it on the next question, not just confirm the key.
26	
27	Complete (full breakdown plus all five Choice Notes) is a gate, not a score.

1	# Main Conclusion: 40 candidates (of 196 complete)
2	
3	
4	=================== PT140-S1-Q18 | diff 2 | key C | score 23.2 | features: Famous Flaws
5	STIMULUS: Mayor: A huge protest against plans to build a chemical plant in this town was held yesterday. The protesters claim that the factory could cause health problems. But this worry can be dismissed. Most of the protesters were there only because they were paid to show up by property developers who are concerned that the factory would lower the value of nearby land that they own.
6	STEM: Which one of the following most accurately expresses a flaw in reasoning in the mayor’s argument?
7	(A) [5% | Not Straw Man] The argument mischaracterizes an opposing view and then attacks this mischaracterized view.
8	(B) [2% | Not Inappropriate Appeal Opposite, if anything] The argument attempts to persuade by inducing fear of the consequences of rejecting its conclusion.
9	(C) [88% | Correct] The argument rejects a claim simply because of the motivation that some people have for making it.
10	(D) [4% | Not Sampling] The argument generalizes on the basis of a few unrepresentative cases.
11	(E) [1% | Not Possible vs. Certain] The argument mistakes a claim that a result is possible for a claim that the result is inevitable.
12	--- BREAKDOWN
13	## Conclusion
14	This worry can be dismissed. rephrase: We can ignore the concern that the chemical plant could cause health problems.
15	## Evidence
16	Most of the people who showed up to protest this plant were only there because they were paid to show up by people who don't want the factory to be built for property-value reasons.
17	## Evaluate
18	Whenever we're doing a Flaw question and we see the author rebutting someone's claim, we want to immediately become suspicious that we might be seeing Ad Hominem or Unproven vs. Proven False (two of the ten famous flaws). With Ad Hominem, we dismiss someone's point of view because they are a biased source ( they have some vested interest in matter) or because they have conflicting past behavior. With Unproven vs. Proven False, we describe how someone's effort to prove X failed (through lack of evidence or flawed argument) and then we illicitly conclude that X is therefore false. So what about this argument? If some people at a protest say "Stop eating Snickers bars. They have a chemical dye that has been traced to cancer!", are we allowed to dismiss it simply by saying, "Ahhh, don't listen to them. They were paid to be there by Butterfinger, who wants to cut into their market share." No. We're not allowed to conclude that what the protesters are saying is factually wrong just because they have a biased reason for saying it. We have to investigate the merits of the position itself — is there any evidence that Snickers does have a chemical that is linked to cancer? Do we have any evidence for arguing that Snickers does not have any carcinogen in it?
19	## Goal
20	Let's look for an answer that is objecting that the author is too harsh is dismissing the concerns of the protesters. Even if they were paid to be there, there may be some legitimacy to their claims. We should address the content of their argument rather than dismiss it because of the source of who's saying it. Or we should responsibly conclude that these protesters are not strong evidence that the factory could cause health problems, but we should otherwise stay agnostic about whether the factory really could cause health problems, unless we're producing evidence of our own.
21	--- CHOICE NOTES
22	(A) This answer refers to a somewhat famous flaw (not top 15) called Straw Man, in which you turn your opponent's position into an unfair version of what was actually said.
23	If someone said, "I think we should raise the minimum wage to $15" and her opponent responded, "Well, I don't think people should get rich for flipping burgers", that would be a straw man position he is arguing against.
24	Here, since the original concern was that "the factory could cause health problems", a straw man would sound something like, "But we can dismiss the concern that everyone who lives near the factory or works there will die of cancer".
25	This argument didn't misrepresent the opponent's position. They just failed to address it all, dismissing their right to have a position based on the fact that someone paid them to be there.
26	(B) This answer refers to one of the ten famous flaws called Inappropriate Appeal, in which the author's argument leans on our sympathies/fears or leans on the opinion of someone who isn't established to be a qualified expert.
27	The evidence here has nothing to do with playing up fears. This author is trying to tamp down fears by saying that the concerns are only being raised disingenuously by people who object to the construction for other reasons.
28	(C) When Flaw answers describe two part reasoning moves, we can try to match up each part with the Evidence or Conclusion.
29	The author [does something in their conclusion] because of [something in their evidence].
30	Was the conclusion rejecting a claim? Definitely, "This worry can be dismissed".
31	Was the evidence talking about the motivation that some people had for making the claim? Yes, the evidence says that "most of the protesters were there only because they were paid to show up".
32	If an answer is descriptively true, we then ask ourselves, "Okay, this is true about the argument. Is it a criticism of the logic?"
33	Yes! You're not allowed to reject a claim because of the motives of the person who said it. Logically, you evaluate whether the claim is true or false by considering the evidence for or against it, which our author never did.
34	(D) This answer refers to one of the ten famous flaws called Sampling, in which we worry about the sample that an argument is based on because it's either too small in size (i.e. like just a handful of data points) or because the sample seems biased, dishonest, or self-selecting.
35	This answer choice is specifically referring to a Sampling flaw in which there are a few data points but the paragraph or our common sense has given us some reason to think that these data points aren't likely to be typical of whatever group the author is talking about.
36	This conclusion is not a generalization at all, so we can immediately ditch this answer when we try to match up that half of the answer. The conclusion is a specific claim that "THIS worry can be dismissed".
37	(E) This answer describes one of the semi-famous flaws (not top 10) known as Possible vs. Certain. When Flaw answer choices say the author "mistakes X for Y" or "confuses X with Y", it will only be correct if the evidence talked about one of those while the conclusion talked about (or acted like we knew) the other one.
38	Is there any claim here about a result being possible?
39	Sure, there's a claim (from the protesters) that the result of the factory's operation could be health problems.

138	(C) [3% | Too Strong: All] All of the paintings by Renoir and Cézanne that are owned by the Federici Art Museum were purchased by Ms. Federici herself.
139	(D) [3% | Too Strong: Only] Only an avid collector of paintings by Cézanne would be willing to pay a high price for early works by this artist.
140	(E) [8% | Too Strong: only if
141	Out of Scope: truly appreciated
142	Out of Scope: well-maintained] A great work of art can be truly appreciated only if it is displayed in a carefully designed and well-maintained gallery.
143	--- BREAKDOWN
144	## Conclusion (Hence)
145	The board's action [selling some works from its collection] will not detract from the quality of the museum's collection.
146	## Evidence
147	The curator says that among the collection are several unsuccessful immature works by Renoir and Cezanne that should be sold because they're of inferior quality and so add nothing to the overall quality of the museum's collection.
148	## Evaluate
149	The key issue we're analyzing is whether or not selling some works from the collection would *detract from the quality of the collection*.
150	  The curator wants to sell some early works by Renoir / Cezanne that are "of inferior quality and **so** add nothing to the overall quality of the museum's collection".
151	  If they truly add *nothing* to the overall quality of the collection, then it seems logically valid to say getting rid of them doesn't detract from the quality of the collection. So we'd have to fight the argument further upstream.
152	  That "**so**" from the curator is technically a conclusion that the curator is deriving; we don't necessarily have to go along with that idea. We could try to argue that even though these early works by Renoir / Cezanne are 'unsuccessful', that doesn't mean that they add nothing to the quality of the collection (maybe part of the quality of the collection involves seeing how these eventual masters started off as unsuccessful painters).
153	## Goal
154	We might see a *Linking* answer like, "If it's a successful immature work or if it's of inferior quality, then it adds nothing to the overall quality of the collection".
155	  More likely, we'll see some *Defender* answer, ruling out a possible objection. If we negate the correct answer, it will weaken the argument, meaning it will help us argue that this action to sell some works from the collection **could** detract from the quality of the collection.
156	--- CHOICE NOTES
157	(A) The author seems to be assuming that "there will still be a market" for these crappy Renoir paintings that the curator wants to sell, but she doesn't need to assume that art buyers **cannot tell** that these are crappy Renoir paintings.
158	
159	Even if they are *able* to distinguish an inferior painting by Renoir, they might still be *willing* to pay a good bit of money for it (since he's a famous painter).
160	(B) If we negate this, it will be saying, "Some of the paintings that the board will be selling are not part of that group of crappy Renoir / Cezanne paintings".
161	
162	Would that weaken?
163	
164	Sure. That gives us a way to argue that the board's action could detract from the quality of the collection. We could say to the author, "Even though those paintings recommended by the curator add noting to the overall quality of the collection (so selling *them* wouldn't detract from the quality of the collection), the board would also be planning to sell *other* works. Those other works might currently add something to the collection, and so selling them might detract from the quality of the collection."
165	
166	It's not the most powerful objection, but it creates a big cloud of doubt. We don't know anything about these other paintings the board would be selling, so we can't any longer have the confidence the author has in her conclusion that we won't be detracting from the collection's quality based on the paintings we sell-off.
167	(C) Does it really matter if Ms. F bought 100% of these paintings herself? If we negate this answer and say that, "At least one of these was bought by Ms. F's nephew", would that hurt the argument?
168	
169	Of course not. Who bought the paintings has nothing to do with whether selling them will affect the quality of the collection.
170	(D) The curator is definitely assuming that there will be some market for these early, inferior Cezanne's, but there's no reason she has to have the extreme idea that **the only possible buyer** is an avid collector.
171	
172	If, in addition to avid collectors, a pawn shop or an art school or a moderately-enthused collector of paintings were interested in buying an early Cezanne, the author's argument wouldn't be hurt at all.
173	
174	In fact, the more possible buyers there are for these early Cezanne's, the better, in terms of the curator's plan and the author's conclusion.
175	(E) This answer is introducing a lot of distinctions we never talked about. There was no discussion of *great art*, nor *true appreciation*, nor the *design* or *condition* of galleries.
176	
177	This answer is apparently directed at the first sentence. It seems to be inviting us to think about what the **Board of Trustees** was assuming, when they decided they should refurbish its galleries. Maybe they were thinking, "This shoddy gallery won't do. Great art can only be appreciated in a well-designed and well-maintained gallery."

446	  The word must is being used to convey opinion in the second sentence. It's a weird word, because in conditional logic contexts it means factual necessity. But in "Solve the Mystery / Explain the Curious Fact" contexts, must is used to convey that speculative use of must that we use.
447	  Pretend you were hosting a party, you left to go to the bathroom, and when you came back you see that two of your friends (a married couple) left without saying goodbye. Your brain would want to Solve the mystery / Explain the Curious Fact.
448	  You might scratch your temple, confused, and say, "Huh ... they must have gotten a call from their babysitter saying that something was wrong with little Avery."
449	  That's the speculative "must" being used in the 2nd sentence of this paragraph. We would hear more naturally as announcing the author's causal hypothesis if it occurred at the end of the paragraph:

451	  So one thing assisting our ability to find this conclusion is our knowledge that the Conclusion almost always occurs earlier than the Support (on Main Conclusion and Role questions). 824	(A) [16% | Fact, not Opinion] Each of many different human hormones can by itself raise blood glucose levels.
825	(B) [62% | Correct] The reason that many different hormones can each independently raise blood glucose levels is probably a metabolic quirk of the brain.
826	(C) [2% | Premise] Although most human cells can produce energy from fats and proteins, brain cells can produce energy only from glucose.
827	(D) [16% | Intermediate Conclusion / Last Claim Trap] If blood glucose levels fall too low, then brain cells starve, resulting in loss of consciousness and death.
828	(E) [4% | Never Said] The reason brain cells starve if deprived of glucose is that they can produce energy only from glucose.
829	--- BREAKDOWN
830	## Conclusion (probably)
831	The reason that every human hormones has the power to raise glucose in the blood is probably a metabolic quirk of the brain.
832	## Evidence (to see this, consider)
833	Brain cells can use only glucose. So if blood glucose falls too low, brain cells will starve, resulting in unconsciousness and death.
834	## Evaluate
835	This is a famously hard Main Conclusion question. Whenever we read Main Conclusion we prime our brain to listen for a Conclusion that comes **earlier** than its Supporting ideas, because 85% or more of the time that's where we find the Conclusion on Main Conclusion questions.

839	The word "probably" in the second sentence indicates that it's an Opinion. The **support indicator** "to see this, consider" shows us that the final two sentences are trying to support this 2nd sentence.
840	The "thus" in the final sentence also indicates either a Conclusion or a Cause/Effect relationship.
841	Conclusion "thus" Sam likes my dog. Thus, Sam must be a dog lover.
842	Causal "thus" Sam fell off his roof and thus broke his leg.
843	Because the last two sentences are telling us biological facts, the final claim about "if no glucose, then death" seems more like a Cause/Effect statement than a reasoning conclusion.
844	But whichever we would call it, it would still just be an Intermediate Conclusion, because the final two sentences stand in support of the 2nd sentence.
845	## Goal
846	Since we've identified the 2nd sentence as the foremost Opinion the author is Supporting, we're just looking for an answer choice that matches the 2nd sentence in meaning.

856	(C) This, again, is presented as an established science fact, not the author's opinion.
857

859	(D) The conclusion on Main Conclusion is usually not found in the final claim (unless it's an easy problem), so we want to be wary before picking an answer that looks like the last claim.
860

865	The author is saying, "Because brains only metabolize glucose and would die without it, that's probably why every hormone has the power to raise glucose levels".

878	(E) [4% | Too Strong / Premise] The more stress a person experiences, the more likely that person is to crave chocolate.
879	--- BREAKDOWN
880	## Conclusion
881	It is likely that common wisdom (in thinking that chocolate causes acne, based on the fact that people often get an outbreak of acne right after they eat a bunch of chocolate) has mistaken an effect for a cause.
882	## Evidence
883	Several recent studies show that hormonal changes associated with stress can cause acne, and there's good evidence that people who are fond of chocolate tend to eat more chocolate when they are under stress.
884	## Evaluate

886	Because of that, if we see the first sentence say "it is widely believed", we can tell we're getting the sort of attribution to someone else's point of view that an author would then proceed to rebut.
887	When we see the Pivot word (but, yet, **however** ), then it starts to definitely look like a rebuttal. The "However" claim does sound like our Author offering her opinion. Does she proceed to support that opinion? Yes, the final sentence is evidence (several recent studies).

893	Look for something like "The fact that chocolate eating is usually followed by an outbreak of acne is probably because both those things are related to stress".

897	She's saying, "You guys might be wrong to think the acne you got was caused by chocolate. It was probably caused by stress."
898	(B) This author has never presented this incredibly strong Volume Dial relationship (the more x, the more y).
899

902	(D) The conclusion isn't saying the original hypothesis (that the acne is caused by the chocolate) is definitely wrong. It's saying that it's likely wrong.
903	
904	Our author's alternate explanation is that both the chocolate and the acne are just symptoms of underlying stress.
905	
906	Because she prefers this explanation to the original one, we can express her belief by saying, "It's more likely that stress is causing both than that chocolate is causing acne".
907	(E) This author has never presented this incredibly strong Volume Dial relationship (the more x, the more y).

1219	There are a lot of moving pieces in this argument, but fortunately there are some clues which help us identify the role played by each statement.

1221	  The word "since" in the final sentence is also a common premise indicator. We're seeing another conclusion – premise relationship in this sentence. "The criminalization of these forms of speech is justified," is a conclusion supported by the evidence which comes after the word "since."
1222	  We seem to have two separate conclusions here, but their meaning is very similar. Saying "the criminalization of some forms of speech is justified" is essentially the same as saying, "the legal right to free speech does not protect all speech." If you wondered for a moment if one of these was an intermediate conclusion, that's a good question to ask. But these statements are similar enough for us to say that they're repeating the same idea.

Since the whole point and purpose of an argument is to present and support the conclusion, this could be the same thing as asking for the politician's conclusion. We'll keep an open mind as we look at the answers, though, just in case one correctly describes the argument in a more general way. Beware of answers that include details which seem relevant but weren't actually discussed in the argument.

1229	(D) The politician never claims that any form of speech leads to serious harm, only that certain forms of speech do.

1236	(A) [2% | Wrong Objection / Unstated] Gillette’s argument wrongly assumes that deciphering the genetic code will lead to cures for genetic disorders.

1238	(C) [1% | Too Strong: should not be conducted] Because of its possible negative consequences, genetic research should not be conducted.
1239	(D) [2% | Wrong Objection / Unstated] Gillette’s claim that a thorough knowledge of genetics would enable us to cure over 3,000 disorders is overstated.
1240	(E) [68% | Correct] Gillette’s argument is unconvincing because it ignores certain possible consequences of genetic research.
1241	--- BREAKDOWN
1242	## Conclusion (however)
1243	Gillette's argument is not persuasive. (it's not clear that a thorough knowledge of genetics would certainly benefit humanity, just because it could cure diseases)
1244	## Evidence (because)
1245	He fails to consider that such thorough knowledge of genetics might harm human beings more than it would benefit them.
1246	## Evaluate
1247	Whenever we're doing a Main Conclusion question, we prime our brains before reading the paragraph with the prevailing tendencies on Main Conclusion questions: Rather than put the Conclusion as the final claim, the conclusion on Main Conclusion questions is usually found earlier than the evidence. Typically, it's ... - the very first claim of the paragraph - it's rebutting some previous or external point of view that isn't the author's In this paragraph we start with an external point of view, Gillette's. Most of the time in LR and RC, after an author attributes a point of view to someone, she pivots away with a but / yet / however. That pivot doesn't necessarily mean we're hearing a conclusion. It could go: However, Conclusion. After all, Support. But it could also go: However, Support. Thus, Conclusion In this paragraph, the word because clearly indicates that the last idea is Support, so that makes "G's argument is not persuasive" our Conclusion. F.A.B.S. = for, after all, because, since (the four Support indicators you should memorize)

1280	The most common place to find an implied conclusion is with a rebuttal argument. When an author starts off with an attribution and then pivots with a **but / yet / however**, then we can pretty safely assume that the conclusion will just sound like it's disagreeing with the original attribution claim.

1286	The "as if" is a sarcastic comment, showing us that the author takes issue with the article's logic. As we read the first claim, it's hard to hear whether it's an opinion or a fact, but as soon as we see "since", we know that the first half of that first sentence is a Conclusion. Any time we see F.A.B.S (for, after all, because, since), it's showing us a supporting idea. Any time we see "since / because" we know the other clause in that sentence is a conclusion. 599	STIMULUS: Obviously, entrepreneurial ability is needed to start a successful company. Yet many entrepreneurs who succeed in starting a company fail later for lack of managerial skills. For instance, they do not adequately analyze market trends and, consequently, they fail in managing company growth. Hence, the lack of managerial skills and the lack of entrepreneurial ability can each inhibit the development of successful companies.
600	STEM: The proposition that certain entrepreneurs fail in managing company growth plays which one of the following roles in the argument above?
601	(A) [2% | Wrong Role] It is the main conclusion drawn in the argument.
602	(B) [7% | Not an Example of Main Conclusion] It is presented as an example of the phenomenon the argument seeks to explain.
603	(C) [1% | Wrong Role] It is meant as an aside and is not supposed to provide evidence in support of the argument’s conclusion.
604	(D) [23% | Not Directly] It is a premise that is intended to support the argument’s main conclusion directly.
605	(E) [68% | Correct] It is an example that is offered in support of a premise that is intended to support the argument’s main conclusion directly.
606	--- BREAKDOWN
607	## Conclusion (Hence)
608	The lack of managerial skills and the lack of entrepreneurial ability can each inhibit the development of successful companies.
609	## Evidence
610	Entrepreneurial ability is obviously needed to start a successful company.
611	## Intermediate Conclusion
612	But even if you succeed in starting a company, you can still fail for lack of managerial skills.
613	## Evidence (for instance)
614	You might not adequately analyze market trends, which leads to a failure to manage company growth.
615	## Evaluate
616	There are lot of keywords here potentially helping us / confusing us: Obviously = opinion. Opinions are often Conclusions. But the first sentence isn't supported by any idea, so we wouldn't call it a conclusion. Yet = pivot. Pivots frequently introduce the main conclusion on Role and Main Conclusion questions. The idea after Yet does get supported, which we can tell from the fact that it's followed by ... For instance = support indicator. consequently = This can indicate a conclusion or the result of a cause/effect relationship. In this sentence, it appears to be a causal use of consequently, not a reasoning move. Hence = conclusion indicator.
617	## Goal
618	We're being asked about a claim that comes right before the word "Hence", so we're guaranteed that this claim plays a supporting role. Other than that, we don't need to be too precise with our prediction.
619	--- CHOICE NOTES
620	(A) The main conclusion is the final claim of the paragraph. A claim could never be the main conclusion if it's followed by the word "Hence", because the "hence" indicates that a conclusion is being drawn on top of that.
621	(B) Is this argument seeking to explain a phenomenon? Not really. That language means "to offer a causal explanation for something that happened / is happening". This argument is only trying to demonstrate that each of two factors has the power to thwart the successful development of a company. If we stretched this answer to fit, we'd say the phenomenon the author seeks to explain is that of "a company failing due to lack of managerial skills or lack of entrepreneurial ability". An example of that phenomenon would be a specific company that failed due to one of these things. The claim we're being asked about certainly isn't providing a specific case study in failure. Our claim is giving an example of how someone's managerial skills could be lacking. But the argument doesn't seek to explain "how someone's managerial skills could be lacking".
622	(C) This portrays our claim as neutral Background info, but we know this claim plays a supporting role. After all, it's followed by the word Hence. Conclusion indicators such as So, Thus, Hence, Therefore always indicate that the claim immediately before them is providing some support.
623	(D) The claim we're being asked about exists in a sentence that is prefaced by the phrase For instance. "For instance / for example" always indicate that sentence we're now reading is supporting the sentence we just read. We know the 3rd sentence is directly supporting the 2nd sentence, and the 2nd sentence is directly supporting the 4th sentence (the main conclusion).
624	(E) The claim we're being asked about exists in a sentence that is prefaced by the phrase For instance. "For instance / for example" always indicate that sentence we're now reading is an example that supports the previous claim. We know the 3rd sentence is offering an example to support the 2nd sentence. 2nd: many entrepreneurs fail for lack of managerial skills. 3rd: for example, they can fail because they lack the managerial skills to adequately manage company growth. Is the 2nd sentence a premise intended to support the argument's main conclusion directly? Yes. The final sentence (the main conclusion) is directly supported by the first two sentences. Sentence 1 establishes that a lack of entrepreneurial skills can tank a business. Sentence 2 establishes that lack of managerial skills can also do so. The 2nd sentence is an Intermediate Conclusion, aka a Subsidiary Conclusion, aka a Supported Premise.

If we see, "Claim 1 and thus Claim 2", that's different. There, Claim 2 is definitely some sort of conclusion or effect of Claim 1. But when we just see "Claim 1 and Claim 2", we assume they are both premises. The last two ideas combine to show why our solar system has an advantage when it comes to favoring the emergence of life.

681	STIMULUS: According to economists, people’s tendency to purchase a given commodity is inversely proportional to its price. When new techniques produced cheaper steel, more steel was purchased. Obviously, then, there are exceptions to the economists’ general rule.
682	STEM: The claim that more steel was purchased when it could be manufactured more cheaply plays which one of the following roles in the argument?
683	(A) [1% | Out of Scope: described as inadequate] It is described as inadequate evidence for the falsity of the argument’s conclusion.
684	(B) [3% | Out of Scope: described / Opposite: exception] It is described as an exception to a generalization for which the argument offers evidence.
685	(C) [86% | Correct] It is used to illustrate the generalization that, according to the argument, does not hold in all cases.
686	(D) [8% | Out of Scope: led economists to embrace] It is the evidence that, according to the argument, led economists to embrace a false hypothesis.
687	(E) [1% | Wrong Role / Out of Scope: modifying] It is cited as one of several reasons for modifying a general assumption made by economists.
688	--- BREAKDOWN
689	## Conclusion (Obviously, then)
690	There are exceptions to the economists' general rule.
691	  economists' rule: Tendency to purchase is inversely proportional to price.
692	  Conclusion-translated: Sometimes people's tendency to purchase a given commodity is not inversely proportional to its price
693	  (i.e. the price rises and more want to buy it, or the price drops and fewer people want to buy it)
694	## Evidence
695	Sure there's an example that fits the economists rule, where price and purchasing were inverted: the price of steel went down, and the amount purchasing went up.
696	  But, there's a counterexample to the rule, where price and purchasing were aligned: the price of lace went down, and the purchasing of lace went down (if you can't rub it in the poor's face that they can't afford lace, then what's the point?)
697	## Evaluate
698	The arguments on Main Conclusion and Role are intentionally more opaque. They don't want to make finding the conclusion an easy part of the process, because it's the main event on these questions. So any time we're doing Main Conclusion or Role, we're anticipating the conclusion probably won't be at the end with an obvious Therefore / Thus indicator word. But sometimes it will be last, usually on easier ranked problems like this Q4.
699	  Instead, the Conclusion will usually be one of these two:
700	  - the first claim in the paragraph
701	  - a rebuttal
702	  In this case the conclusion is at the end of the paragraph, and it does have some indicator words, although they're less obvious ones.
703	  Obviously / evidently / clearly / apparently are frequently used on LSAT to show us an opinion, and of course when we're searching for the Main Conclusion we're searching for the opinion that the author supports.
704	  Then / consequently / as a result are used to show the terminal end of a chain of thought, i.e. the conclusion of a train of thought.
705	  This final sentence is the author's rebuttal, so if we're familiar with looking for Rebuttal Conclusions and First Sentence Conclusions on Main Conclusion and Role, we'll hopefully gravitate towards the rebuttal, since the first sentence contained an attribution to "economists".
706	  The claim we're being asked about is the author's concession to the occasional validity of the economists point of view.
707	  The four claims here go like this:
708	  1. Attributed claim
709	  "according to economists, X"
710	  2. Author's conceding some validity
711	  "here's an example about steel that matches X"
712	  3. Author pivoting into premise
713	  "Nevertheless, here's a counterexample to X"
714	  4. Author's rebuttal conclusion
715	  "Obviously, then, the economists are wrong"
716	## Goal
717	They're asking us about #2, so we want something like, "The author is acknowledging that there are some data points that do fit the economists' general rule (even though the rule does not universally hold)."
718	--- CHOICE NOTES
719	(A) It isn't described as anything, so that's reason enough to consider this answer inaccurate.
720	
721	The argument's conclusion is saying "there are exceptions to Rule X" and the claim we're looking at is an example of a data point that conforms to Rule X.
722	
723	If an author is trying to prove that "there are exceptions to Rule X", a data point that matches rule X isn't evidence for the conclusion's falsity. It's really just irrelevant. If we're trying to prove that there are exceptions, our argument has nothing to do with whether or not there are conforming examples.
724	(B) It isn't described as anything, so that's reason enough to consider this answer inaccurate.
725	
726	The argument's conclusion is saying "there are exceptions to generalization X", but the claim we're looking at is an example of a data point that conforms to the generalization.
727	(C) Is there a generalization that, according to the argument, does not hold in call cases?

734	(D) Is there something that, according to the argument, led economists to embrace a false hypothesis?

744	=================== PT127-S1-Q21 | diff 4 | key D | score 18.9 | features: Science
745	STIMULUS: Scientist: Isaac Newton’s Principia, the seventeenth-century work that served as the cornerstone of physics for over two centuries, could at first be understood by only a handful of people, but a basic understanding of Newton’s ideas eventually spread throughout the world. This shows that the barriers to communication between scientists and the public are not impermeable. Thus recent scientific research, most of which also can be described only in language that seems esoteric to most contemporary readers, may also become part of everyone’s intellectual heritage.
746	STEM: Which one of the following most accurately describes the role played in the scientist’s argument by the claim that recent scientific research can often be described only in language that seems esoteric to most contemporary readers?
747	(A) [5% | Out of Scope: truth called into doubt] It is raised as a potential objection to the argument’s main conclusion, but its truth is called into doubt by the preceding statements.
748	(B) [5% | Too Strong: only superficially different] It is a premise that supports the argument’s main conclusion by suggesting that the results of recent scientific research are only superficially different from claims made in Newton’s Principia.
749	(C) [20% | Wrong Role] It is cited as further evidence for the conclusion that the barriers to communication between scientists and the public are not impermeable.
750	(D) [69% | Correct] It is a claim that serves mainly to help establish the relevance of the preceding statements to the argument’s final conclusion.
751	(E) [0% | Opposite] It serves to cast doubt on an alleged similarity between Newton’s Principia and recent scientific research.
752	--- BREAKDOWN
753	## Conclusion (Thus)
754	Recent scientific research may also become part of everyone's intellectual heritage.
755	## Counterpoint
756	Even though recent scientific research requires language that seems esoteric (hard to understand) to most modern readers,
757	## Intermediate Conc (this shows that)
758	Barriers to communication between scientists and the public are not impermeable.
759	## Evidence
760	Netwon's Principia was not understandable to most of its contemporaneous audience, but a basic understanding of Newton's ideas eventually spread globally and it served as the cornerstone of physics for over two centuries.
761	## Evaluate
762	They're asking us about the grammatically inessential modifier in the final sentence, which contains our Main Conclusion. When a Main Conclusion packages in a noun modifier, we treat that modifier as a fact. For example, given this conclusion ... Thus, Dan's attorney, who went to Harvard, is clearly capable of solving this tax problem. .... we would think that it's being factually presented to us that Dan's attorney went to Harvard, whereas the Conclusion is presenting the speculative opinion that she would clearly be capable of solving this tax problem. The claim from the last sentence that they're asking us about is technically an Opposing idea, since the conclusion is that "recent science may one day be part of everyone's intellectual heritage" whereas the claim we're being asked about is saying "recent science is inscrutably confusing to most modern readers". That undermines the idea that this modern science will ultimately be absorbed into our common wisdom. However, the author's argument is trying to show by analogy that science that is initially too confusing for the masses (like Newton's was) can still eventually be accepted.
763	## Goal
764	Let's look for something like, "a potential objection to the conclusion that the author argues, by analogy, is a surmountable problem".
765	--- CHOICE NOTES
766	(A) The first half of this is close to being fine, but the second half has no support. The author is never calling into doubt the truth of the claim that "modern science is wicked confusing to most people".
767	She is fully accepting the truth of that idea and saying, "But don't worry. They felt the same about Newton as we do about our modern science. Eventually a basic understanding might spread. There won't necessarily always be this barrier to understanding."
768	(B) The author is saying that recent scientific research has one thing in common with Newton's Principia — they both at first can only be understood by a small group of knowledgeable science type people.
769	But it's way stronger to say that recent research is only superficially different from Newton's claims (i.e. they are essentially the same claims).
770	(C) Our claim is not evidence. It doesn't have a Supporting relationship to the main conclusion. It is a potential reason to doubt the conclusion, but it is countered by the intermediate conclusion that "the barriers to communication between scientists and the public are not impermeable".
771	(D) This answer doesn't identify the relationship between our claim and the final conclusion (our claim somewhat undermines the plausibility of the final conclusion).
772	But it accurately says that our claim is one of the key ways that the author is making her argument by analogy.
773	If we were never told that recent scientific research can only be understood by a small circle of people, then we would have no idea why we talked about Newton's Principia in the preceding sentences.
774	If that final sentence just said, "Thus recent scientific research may also become part of everyone's heritage", we'd wonder what the connection was to the previous two sentences ... are you saying that recent research is also something that can at first be understood by only a handful of people?
775	(E) Our claim is meant to provide the underlying similarity between Newton's Principia and recent scientific research, not to cast doubt on the similarity.

My conclusion was there was that Kevin is really crying because of the new climate change warnings, but I didn't actually support that with anything. 78	STIMULUS: Edgar: Some of the pumps supplying water to our region have been ordered shut down in order to protect a species of small fish. But it is absurd to inconvenience thousands of people for the sake of something so inconsequential.
79	Rafaela: You’re missing the point. The threat to that fish species is a sign of a very serious threat to our water supply.
80	STEM: The dialogue provides the most support for the claim that Edgar and Rafaela disagree over whether
81	(A) [3% | Unsupported Disagree Position] shutting down the pumps will actually inconvenience a large number of people
82	(B) [72% | Correct] the survival of the fish species is the only reason for shutting down the pumps
83	(C) [24% | Unsupported Disagree Position] species of small fish are inconsequential
84	(D) [0% | Out of Scope: legal] the order to shut down the pumps was legal
85	(E) [0% | Too Strong: sufficient] shutting down the pumps will be sufficient to protect the fish species
86	--- BREAKDOWN
87	## Edgar's Claims
88	  1. Some of the pumps supplying water to our region were shut down to protect a small fish species.
89	  2. It's absurd to inconvenience thousands of people for the sake of something so inconsequential.
90	## Rafaela's Responses
91	  1. No comment, but she seems to implicitly agree with this, since she's telling Edgar what she knows about this situation of shutting down the pumps to protect this fish.
92	  2. She doesn't directly disagree with this, because she isn't saying, "It's not absurd; it's perfectly reasonable to inconvenience thousands for something so inconsequential."
93	## Evaluate
94	So since the 2nd speaker isn't disagreeing with any of the explicit claims, we need to think about some of the implicit assumptions the 1st speaker was making. Edgar is assuming that "shutting down some of the pumps" is going to "inconvenience thousands of people". And he's assuming that "this species of small fish" is "something inconsequential". Rafaela seems to be disagreeing with the idea of "inconsequential", because her wording of "a very serious threat" is the best contrast we can find between her and Edgar. However, is Rafaela saying that this fish is a very consequential species, and thus worth taking drastic steps to save? Not really. It sounds like she's saying, "The water professionals are looking at this small fish and thinking, oh crap! If this fish is suffering then that means something in the water is hurting it, and whatever that thing is could potentially hurt humans too, since it could be in our water supply." So it seems like Rafaela might be disagreeing with Edgar's understanding that the pumps are being shut down in order to protect the small fish. She seems to think that the pumps are being shut down in order to investigate the potential pollutant that is threatening this fish and could one day threaten us.
95	## Goal
96	Look for something like "whether the pumps are being shut down to protect the fish or to do some investigative work in the hopes of protecting humans".
97	--- CHOICE NOTES
98	(A) Edgar would Agree with this, but we have no support for the Disagree position.
99	From Rafaela's statements, we can't derive that she thinks, "Shutting down the pumps won't inconvenience lots of people".
100	(B) This is the only answer speaking to why we're shutting down the pumps, which was what our prediction was all about.
101	It comes off sounding very extreme, but Edgar is definitely under the impression that the pumps are being shut down for the sake of this fish (and only for that). That's why he's so exasperated that we're going to such inconvenient lengths just for the sake of this one, small fish species.
102	Meanwhile, Rafaela would support the Disagree position. She thinks that there's at least one reason (other than protecting the small fish) that they are shutting down the pumps: they're worried that whatever in the water is harming this fish could later harm us, by entering our water supply.
103	(C) Edgar would somewhat Agree with this, because he's assuming that this small fish species is inconsequential, but this answer sounds more like a universal, "ALL species of small fish are inconsequential". It's not clear that Edgar believes that.
104	More importantly, though, we have no support for the Disagree position. From Rafaela's statements, we can't derive that she thinks, "species of small fish are consequential".
105	She's never arguing that this small fish we're talking about is consequential. She's saying whatever it is that's harming this small fish could be consequential.
106	(D) Neither one of these people speak to the legality of the shut down, so we wouldn't be able to support the Agree or Disagree position.
107	(E) Neither one of these people speak to the sufficiency of the shut down, so we wouldn't be able to support the Agree or Disagree position.
108	This answer is saying that "if we shut down the pumps, it's 100% certain the fish species will be protected". We can't derive that from either set of statements.

118	(C) [3% | Unsupported Both Too Strong: primary] Reporters’ primary responsibility is to see that people are kept informed of the facts.
119	(D) [5% | No Support for Person 1] Reporters should not allot time to reporting some events rather than others.
120	(E) [62% | Correct] Reporting on certain events rather than others qualifies as interpreting the news.
121	--- BREAKDOWN
122	## Sarah's 1st Claim
123	By deciding which events will get coverage / airtime, reporters are exercising their judgment as to what is newsworthy and what is not. Does Ramon argue that'deciding which stories to cover is not reporters' exercising their judgment'?
124	No, in fact Ramon seems to agree that reporters exercise their judgment by saying "once [reporters]deem a story to be newsworthy".
125	## Sarah's 2nd Claim
126	Reporters always interpret the news.Does Ramon argue that 'no,sometimes reporters do not interpret the news'?
127	Yes, kind of. He's saying that they should never interpret the news, which isn't a statement about what reporters do; it's a statement about what they should do.
128	But it seems to imply that they have a choice whether or not to interpret the news.
129	## Evaluate
130	This is a weird set of statements to digest. Ramon doesn't quite disagree with either of Sarah's claims, but he definitely doesn't vibe with "they always interpret the news".
131	He's saying, "they should never interpret the news".
132	How are they ending up at such polar extremes (always vs. never)?
133	Well,she's saying that they're interpreting the news by deciding which stories 'to platform', which ones get airtime. Interpretation is defined as 'picking which stories get to be told'.
134	But Ramon is apparently thinking of interpretation of 'deciding how to tell a story' (not deciding to tell a story). We know this because he's saying, "they should tell me the story in untainted facts, not skewed by their interpretation."
135	He's thinking of interpretation as "the subjective spin you put on a story", while she's thinking of it as "the subjectivity involved in picking a story to report on".
136	## Goal
137	It seems like we want an answer that sounds something like, "What does it mean to interpret the news?"
138	--- CHOICE NOTES
139	(A) This answer is very tempting, but Ramon doesn't comment at all on what reporters actually do, only what they should / are obliged to do.
140	
141	Since we aren't told his stance on what they actually do, we can't say he disagrees with Sarah.
142	
143	I also think this answer misrepresents the sense of "always interpreting" that Sarah is using. She would really say "reporters actually interpret the news every time they decide on a story to report".
144	
145	The act of relaying the story isn't even in Sarah's definition of "interpretation". So we might struggle to even say that Sarah agrees with this (and she would be the one more likely to agree).
146	(B) This is the flipside of choice (A). Ramon talked about what reporters should do, but Sarah only talks about what reporters do do.
147	
148	We have no idea what Sarah thinks reporters should do.
149	(C) We couldn't infer from either person's statements what they think is the primary responsibility of reporters.
150	
151	Ramon definitely thinks that deeming a story to be newsworthy and relaying that story in a detached, objective manner are each responsibilities of reporters. Which does he think is a bigger responsibility? Who knows.
152	(D) As soon as we see "should" we can ditch the answer, since Person 1 doesn't make any claims that deal with what should be, just claims that deal with what is the case.
153	(E) Sarah would say YES, because this is the connective tissue between her two statements. The "in other words" means that we could restate the first sentence (deciding what qualifies as news) as "interpreting the news".
154	
155	Ramon would say NO, because he has said that "reporters should never interpret the news". If he thought that "Interpreting news = deciding which stories to report on", then he would be saying "Reporters should never decide which stories to report on".
156	
157	But his 2nd claim begins, "Once reporters decide which stories to report on (i.e. deem which stories are newsworthy".
158	
159	So if he subscribed to this definition of interpreting than he would be contradicting himself: "Reporters should never do X. Once they do X, they should do Y."

161	Instead, Ramon clearly interprets interpreting as failing to relay the facts untainted ... i.e. adding some subjective color to the way they relay a story.

Greta: We should stop funding the museum, not the television programming, because, as the mayor has also pointed out, the museum reaches a much smaller audience.
166	STEM: On the basis of their statements, it can be inferred that Alissa and Greta disagree on which one of the following?
167	(A) [4% | No Support from Either] whether the city will need to cease funding local children’s television programming if it continues funding the children’s museum
168	(B) [1% | No Support from Either] whether the mayor has spoken truthfully about what will need to happen if the city does not cease funding local children’s television programming
169	(C) [75% | Correct] whether the city should cease funding local children’s television programming if continuing to fund it would mean that the city would have to cease funding the children’s museum
170	(D) [19% | No Support from Person 1] whether local children’s television programming provides a beneficial educational experience to a greater number of children in the city than does the children’s museum
171	(E) [1% | No Support from Person 2] whether the children’s museum provides a rich educational experience for those children who visit it
172	--- BREAKDOWN
173	## Alissa's 1st Claim
174	If the city can no longer fund both the kids museum and kids TV programming, it should stop funding the TV programming. Does Greta disagree? Is she arguing that if the city needs to make a choice, they should choose to keep TV programming and stop funding the kids museum? Yes! She explicitly says that.
175	## Alissa's 2nd Claim
176	The interactive character of the exhibits makes for a richer (more active) educational experience than watching TV, which is largely passive. Does Greta disagree? Is she arguing that the exhibits at the museum are not more rich and active than passively watching TV? No, she doesn't talk about that comparison at all.
177	## Evaluate
178	We know that Greta disagrees with Alissa's 1st claim and is silent on Alissa's 2nd claim. In fact, the disagreement is so apparent and explicit, that I would suspect the answers will go one layer deeper. When there is a very obvious and explicit disagreement, then the correct answer usually compares the reasons each person had for reaching their position. Person 1: I think Harvard is the best law school because its grads are most likely to be President or appointed to SCOTUS. Person 2: I think Stanford is the best law school because its grads make the highest average salary in their first job. Rather than giving us an easy, superficial answer like, They disagree over which law school is the best the correct answer will compare reasons, like Average first job salary is a better determinant of a law school's quality than is likelihood of holding prestigious government positions
179	## Goal

184	(B) Just like (A), this is discussing some background issue that neither person comments on.
185	These two people are disagreeing about what we should do if the mayor has spoken truthfully. Neither one of them offers any opinion about whether the mayor has in fact spoken truthfully.
186	To the extent that they both seem to be implicitly trusting what the mayor says, it seems like they would both agree that the mayor is being honest.
187	(C) Alissa would say that,
188	if continuing to fund kids TV would mean that the city would have to cease funding the museum, then we should cease funding kids tv.
189	Greta would say that,
190	if continuing to fund kids TV would mean that the city would have to cease funding the museum, then we should not cease funding kids TV ("we should stop funding the museum not (stop funding) the television programming").
191	This correct answer ended up testing the superficial, obvious disagreement of "Keep the museum vs. No, keep the kids TV", but they just found a really weird way to structure that debate.
192	The reason this answer is structured as a conditional is that they're arguing about Alissa's first claim, which is a conditional!
193	(D) Alissa never talks about the audience size of museum vs. kids programming, so we couldn't infer her opinion on this matter.
194	(E) This is Alissa's second claim, which Greta never acknowledged or pushed back against.
195	We have no support for what Greta's opinion is on whether the museum does / doesn't provide a rich educational experience.

206	(D) [13% | Disagree] Many audience members at comedy venues enjoy watching stand-up comedians respond to heckling.
207	(E) [10% | Disagree] It is unwise for comedy venues to disregard long-standing traditions of stand-up comedy.

209	## Finding the Disagreement
210	On this (unusual) question, there will be four points of disagreement. Let's do our usual process of asking, for each one of Person 1's explicit claims, whether Person 2 argued the opposite.
211	  Did Chapman argue:
212	  heckling is not a long-standing tradition of stand-up comedy?
213	  No, he agreed with that. They probably disagree about why it's a long-standing tradition (McKee seems to feel like it's embraced by the performers, whereas Chapman is saying it's only tolerated by the performers but they wish it weren't happening).
214	  Did Chapman argue:
215	  performers are not aware that heckling is a tradition,
216	  or that performers do not learn to respond entertainingly.
217	  There's no comment on whether performers are aware of the tradition. We could potentially say they disagree when it comes to the entertainment value of heckling.
218	  McKee thinks that the crowd ends up enjoying the heckling, since the comedian is trained to respond entertainingly. Chapman thinks that heckling is usually only enjoyed by the heckler.
219	  Did Chapman argue:
220	  it would be wise for comedy venues to prevent heckling?
221	  Not explicitly, but he is saying that most of the time heckling is just a distraction, so he would be definitely headed in the direction of "let's prohibit it".
222	## Goal
223	It's hard to predict four different disagreements, but we have at least a couple:
224	  - whether or not heckling is fun for everyone or just the heckler
225	  - whether clubs should try to stop it
226	  - whether performers enjoy it or just tolerate it
227	--- CHOICE NOTES
228	(A) We don't have explicit disagreement from Chapman, but on this question, we're going off a standard of "most supportable".
229	
230	McKee thinks clubs should not try to prevent heckling (i.e. they should tolerate it).
231	Chapman would potentially say they should not tolerate it, because it's mostly a distraction from the performance.
232	(B) There is only contextual support for either party's take on this, but it seems to speak to the thrust of their disagreement.
233	
234	McKee thinks that heckling is a time honored tradition; comics are ready for it and use to entertain the audience. Why, it's practically part of the show!
235	
236	Chapman is saying, "No, comics are aware it might happen, but they don't want it to happen. They tolerate it and try to humorously deflect, but it's usually just fun for the heckler and distracting for everyone else".
237	(C) Neither author makes it seem like being able to handle hecklers well is a trait of the best comedians, but it's fair to think that McKee would agree with this.
238	
239	Since he thinks that dealing entertainingly with hecklers is part of the job of being a comedian, he would think that someone good at the job would be able to entertainingly deal with hecklers.
240	
241	But Chapman would potentially agree with this. There isn't any support for Chapman saying that "great stand-ups are unable to respond entertainingly to a heckler".
242	
243	Chapman is acting like, "Sure, maybe they still get a laugh from dealing with the heckler, but they don't want to be dealing with the heckler."
244	(D) This gets to the issue of McKee saying "it's part of the tradition; the performers respond entertainingly (the crowd enjoys). Comedy clubs should allow it."
245	
246	Meanwhile, Chapman is saying "it's tolerated, not enjoyed. It's a distraction from the performance", and most relevantly, "it's usually only fun for the heckler".
247	
248	Technically, Chapman's statements leave plenty of room for him to agree with this answer choice, but when we're comparing our five options, this at least has some support for disagreement, whereas the correct answer (C) had no support for disagreement.
249	(E) This is really a copy of (A) — whether clubs should / shouldn't tolerate heckling.

253	Meanwhile, Chapman thinks heckling is mostly a distraction enjoyed only by the heckler, so he would be fine with comedy clubs disregarding this longstanding tradition.

371	(B) [15% | No Support from Either] whether people who were in their teens or early twenties during the 1960s can write well about the rock music of that period
372	(C) [1% | No Support from Either] whether only people who are past their early twenties can write well about ancient cultures
373	(D) [8% | Both Agree] whether people who are not now in their teens or early twenties can write well about the rock music of the 1960s
374	(E) [2% | Out of Scope: appeal / No Support from Either] whether Ruth's ideas about the rock music of the 1960s are likely to appeal to people who were in their teens or early twenties during that period
375	--- BREAKDOWN

377	You won't be able to write well about the rock music of the 1960s, since you were an infant then. Does Ruth disagree? Does she argue she would be able to write well about rock in the 60s, even though she was an infant then?
378	Yes! First of all she says, "Your **reasoning** is absurd", so we know that she objects to the conclusion drawn on the basis of the evidence ( **since** you were an infant, can't write well).

380	## Jorge's 2nd claim
381	60s rock was created by and for people who were in their teens and early 20s then. Does Ruth disagree? Does she argue that 60's rock was not created by and for people who were in their teens and early 20s at the time?
382	No. She doesn't speak at all to who created 60s rock or for whom 60s rock was created.

386	(A) Jorge would agree with this: "YES, only people who were in their teens or early 20s during the 60s can write well about the rock music of that period (because it was created by and for people who were in their teens / early 20s)."
387	
388	Ruth would disagree with this: "NO, that's crazy. Other people can also be qualified to write well about rock music from the 60s. (after all, people alive today write well about ancient Roman culture, even though they weren't alive to experience it)."
389	
390	We might initially worry that "whether or not you're qualified to write about X" is a different meaning from "whether or not you'd be able to write well about X".
391	
392	They definitely don't seem identical to me, but these answers don't have to be perfect. They just have to be the best available. If we were to ask, "Who is qualified to write about racism in 1980s America?", what would we mean by qualified?
393	
394	Obviously, anyone can write about it, so it doesn't just mean "are you able to write". It means something more like "are you able to understand the subject well enough to offer insight or correct perspective on it", and that's fairly synonymous with "are you able to write well about it"?
395	(B) Neither person discusses whether people in their teens / 20's at the time could have written well about 1960's rock. If we can't derive their opinions on a claim, we can't pick that answer.

397	If we were to speculate, though, we would assume that both people would agree to this claim.
