466	=================== PT109-S4-Q10 | diff 4 | key E | score 15.1 | features: 2-Speaker
467	STIMULUS: Critic: Some writers have questioned Stalin’s sanity
468	during his last years. They typically characterized his vindictiveness and secrecy as “paranoia” and “morbid suspiciousness,” the latter almost a standard term applied by the Soviet writers under glasnost to explain this extraordinary man’s misdeeds. But Stalin’s cruelty and deviousness are not more apparent during those years than in earlier periods of his rule. “Morbid suspiciousness” has to be a characteristic of tyrants. Without it they would not remain long in power.
469	STEM: Which one of the following most accurately expresses a point of disagreement between the critic and the writers?
470	(A) [1% | Unrelated to Goal: should be held guilty] whether Stalin should be held guilty of the cruel deeds attributed to him
471	(B) [17% | Close, but Mismatched Terms] whether Stalin’s cruel misdeeds provide evidence of morbid suspiciousness
472	(C) [10% | Not Choosing Better Reason] whether it is Stalin’s state of paranoia or rather his cruelty that gives the stronger reason for doubting his sanity
473	(D) [3% | Both Agree] whether tyranny tends to lead to cruelty
474	(E) [69% | Correct] whether it was Stalin’s psychological state or rather his political condition that was the primary cause of his cruel misdeeds
475	--- BREAKDOWN
476	## Some Writers
477	1. Stalin was debatably sane during his last years. 2. His vindictiveness (his cruelty) was due to his paranoia and morbid suspiciousness.
478	## Critic
479	Stalin was not especially showing paranoia and morbid suspiciousness in his later years. Stalin was paranoid and morbidly suspicious from the jump (just like any long-lasting tyrant).
480	## Evaluate
481	Where is the critic diverging from the writers? She's saying that it's odd to justify a claim about Stalin's loss of sanity during his waning years, by pointing to the idea that these years were characterized by Stalin being paranoid about people trying to subvert him, and retaliating viciously.
482	After all, that not only characterizes Stalin's early and middle periods as well, but it characterizes any tyrant who wants to stay in power for a long time.
483	## Goal
484	Let's look for something like, "they're arguing about whether Stalin's vindictiveness in his later years were something new and noteworthy vs. something that had been going on for a while" or "whether his vindictiveness in his later years is compelling vs. weak evidence for a loss in sanity".
485	p.s. glasnost is a Russian word meaning "openness", that also was a shorthand for describing the end of the Soviet Union / Cold War in the late 1980s, under Mikhail Gorbachev. The other word from that time, perestroika, meant "change". It was sort of a transition period where Gorbachev tried to move Russia out of its Soviet Union, imperial, communist empire into a slightly more democratic, more Western, more worldly entity. So "writers under glasnost" would have had the motivation of being apologists for how scandalously cruel the Stalin regime was. They would be trying to make Russia not seem as crazy as Stalin would make it look on the world stage, and thus "explain away" his atrocities by saying he was just a confused old man losing his sanity, not the logical conclusion of an authoritarian communist system.
486	--- CHOICE NOTES
487	(A) The dispute is just over whether Stalin was always paranoid and mean, or whether he got especially that way during his later years as his sanity waned.
488	
489	There is no talk here of one side saying Stalin should be held guilty while another side is saying he shouldn't be held guilty for his deeds.
490	(B) The dispute is over whether Stalin's cruel misdeeds and morbid suspiciousness in his later years provide evidence of him going insane.
491	(C) This makes the debate out to be, "THEY think that Stalin's paranoia is the best reason for doubting his sanity" whereas "SHE thinks that Stalin's cruelty is the best reason for doubting his sanity".
492	
493	But the debate was, "THEY think that Stalin may have been losing his sanity in his later years since he was cruel, paranoid, and morbidly suspicious", whereas "SHE thinks that he was cruel, paranoid, and morbidly suspicious during his entire long standing rule, as all tyrants are, so that wouldn't really be evidence that something changed in his later years".
494	(D) They both think that tyranny at least sometimes leads to cruelty. They aren't fighting over whether it tends do (whether it's more than 50% of the time or less than 50% of the time).
495	(E) The wording here is surprising, but we can make this answer fit the disagreement better than any other answer.
496	
497	The Soviet writers used Stalin's psychological state to explain his cruel misdeeds. "Yes, yes, he was committing terrible acts, but it was just because he was losing his sanity in his later years and getting really paranoid and morbidly suspicious of everyone."
498	
499	The critic doesn't buy that storyline. "Stalin was always this way, not just in his later years. He didn't suddenly become mean at the end. Instead, he was always this way because that's how tyrants need to be in order to retain their power. They have to aggressively seek out and snuff any attempts to subvert their rule, so they are all necessarily paranoid and suspicious as a result of their autocratic political position."

503	STIMULUS: Goswami: I support the striking workers at Ergon Foods. They are underpaid. The majority of them make less than $20,000 per year. Nordecki: If pay is the issue, I must disagree. The average annual salary of the striking workers at Ergon Foods is over $29,000.
504	STEM: Goswami and Nordecki disagree over the truth of which one of the following statements?
505	(A) [9% | No Support from Person 1] The average annual salary at Ergon Foods is over $29,000.
506	(B) [11% | No Support from Either] Pay is the primary issue over which the workers are striking at Ergon Foods.
507	(C) [2% | Both Probably Agree] It is reasonable to support striking workers who are underpaid.
508	(D) [72% | Correct] The striking workers at Ergon Foods are underpaid.
509	(E) [5% | No Support from Person 2] It was unreasonable for the workers at Ergon Foods to go on strike.
510	--- BREAKDOWN
511	## Goswami's 1st Claim
512	I support the striking workers at Ergon Foods. Does Nordecki disagree?
513	Almost, but not quite. He says, "If pay is the issue, I must disagree". But that leaves room for the idea that Nordecki would potentially support the strike based on other issues (f.e. working conditions).
514	## Goswami's 2nd Claim
515	The workers at Ergon Foods are underpaid. Does Nordecki disagree?
516	Yes, he seems to disagree. He's saying, "If pay is the issue (for striking), I must disagree (with the decision to strike)", which implies "I don't find their pay objectionably low. I don't consider them underpaid." Also, Nordecki's clarification is all about how much they're paid. He's saying, "Hey -- you're quoting this $20k number, but the average salary of the workers who are striking is over $29k."
517	## Goswami's 3rd Claim
518	The majority of the striking workers make less than $20k per year. Does Nordecki disagree?
519	No, cites a different fact about their pay, but the two facts don't contradict each other. They're just different ways of looking at pay. Goswami is talking about majority pay whereas Nordecki is talking about average pay.
520	## Evaluate / Goal
521	Nordecki says it most clearly, when he says, "If pay is the issue, **I must disagree**." So he disagrees about pay being the issue. He seems to not think the pay these striking workers receive is as worrisome as Goswami does, reinforced by his premise that "if you look at average pay, it's not as bad as you're making it sound".
522	So they're disagreeing over whether "the rate at which the striking workers are paid is good reason to support this strike".
523	--- CHOICE NOTES
524	(A) We know that N agrees with this (he explicitly said it). But we don't have any ammunition to support that G would disagree.
525	
526	We can't point to anything that sounds like G saying, "the average salary is **not** over $29,000". G just talked about most workers making less than $20k, but that's still compatible with average pay being over $29k.
527	(B) We can't tell from either person's statements what the **primary** issue is for why workers are striking. We only know that G's primary reason for supporting the workers is pay, but that doesn't mean that the striking workers consider this the primary reason for why they're striking.
528	
529	We could fix this answer by saying, "Pay is the primary issue that should motivate someone to support these striking workers". G would agree and N would disagree with that.
530	(C) It would be too hard to infer that either person is disagreeing with this, because that person would be saying, "It is unreasonable to support striking workers who are underpaid".
531	
532	Nordecki is the one closer to sounding like that, but he isn't saying that it's unreasonable to support striking workers. He's saying these workers aren't underpaid. Their average salary is over $29k.
533	(D) We know that G agrees because she explicitly says "They (the striking workers) are underpaid."
534	
535	We can support that N disagrees because he says, "Well, if pay is your concern, I'm out. (I'm not worried about pay. After all... ) the average salary of the striking workers is over $29k." p.s. I know this is test 30, which was 20 years ago, but it's weird that LSAC wants us to hear "Hey they make over $29k" as "Hey, they're not underpaid".
536	(E) We know that G would disagree with this. She supports the striking workers, so she implicitly thinks it was reasonable for them to go on strike.
537	
538	We can't say that N would agree with this, though. He only speaks on a conditional basis. "If **pay** is the issue, then I think the strike is unreasonable." But maybe pay wasn't the only or even the main issue.
539	
540	So it's too sweeping of a claim for us to support that N has already condemned this whole strike as unreasonable.

546	STIMULUS: Steven: The allowable blood alcohol level for drivers should be cut in half. With this reduced limit, social drinkers will be deterred from drinking and driving, resulting in significantly increased highway safety.
547	
548	Miguel: No, lowering the current allowable blood alcohol level would have little effect on highway safety, because it would not address the most important aspect of the drunken driving problem, which is the danger to the public posed by heavy drinkers, who often drive with a blood alcohol level of twice the current legal limit.

551	(B) [7% | Both Probably Agree] There is a direct correlation between a driver's blood alcohol level and the driver's ability to drive safely.
552	(C) [6% | Both Probably Agree] A driver with a blood alcohol level above the current legal limit poses a substantial danger to the public.
553	(D) [18% | Disagreement is Extreme] Some drivers whose blood alcohol level is lower than the current legal limit pose a danger to the public.
554	(E) [9% | Agreement is Extreme] A driver with a blood alcohol level slightly greater than half the current legal limit poses no danger to the public.
555	--- BREAKDOWN
556	## Steven's 1st idea
557	We should cut in half the allowable blood alcohol limit for drivers.
558	     *Does M disagree?*
559	  Not sure. Miguel doesn't get into any should / shouldn't language. He's only concerned with what would / wouldn't transpire if we *did* lower blood alcohol level.
560	## Steven's 2nd idea
561	With a lowered limit, social drinkers will be deterred from drinking and driving
562	     *Does M disagree?*
563	  Not sure. Miguel never comments on social drinkers.
564	## Steven's 3rd idea
565	If social drinkers are deterred from drinking and driving, there will be a significant increase in highway safety.
566	     *Does M disagree?*
567	  Yes. Miguel is saying it will have little effect on highway safety (since heavy drinkers are the biggest danger and a reduced limit wouldn't have any effect on them).
568	## Goal
569	We seem to be arguing over whether or not lowering the blood alcohol limit would significantly increase highway safety.
570	  Steven thinks YES, because social drinkers will be deterred.
571	Miguel thinks NO, because it won't affect heavy drinkers, and they are the most important part of the drunk driving problem.
572	--- CHOICE NOTES
573	(A) Steven would say YES, because he thinks that if we lower the blood alcohol limit, thereby deterring social drinkers from drunk driving, we will **significantly** improve highway safety.
574	
575	If reducing drunk driving by social drinkers would significantly improve highway safety, then it must be that drunk driving by social drinkers currently poses a significant threat to highway safety.
576	
577	Migeul would say NO, because he thinks deterring social drinkers **would have little effect** on highway safety, and he explicitly identifies the *most important* aspect of the drunken driving problem as heavy drinkers.
578	
579	Technically, that 2nd idea is not disagreeing with the language of "substantial". Cancer is not **the most common cause of death** (in a non-COVID world, heart disease is), but it's still a **substantial** cause of death.

581	But the 1st idea shows clear disagreement. If eliminating social drunk driving "would have little effect", then social drunk driving does not pose a substantial threat currently.
582	
583	There is a tendency in Disagree questions that if the disagreement is very obvious and explicit (lowering limit will greatly increase safety vs. lowering limit will not greatly increase safety), then the answer ends up testing the underlying rationale for the opposing positions, rather than the opposing positions themselves.

585	
586	Miguel wasn't saying that "lowering the blood alcohol limit will have little effect" because he thinks that blood alcohol is unrelated to driving performance.
587	
588	He was saying lowering the limit will have little effect because the people with the highest blood alcohol level (heavy drinkers) are already ignoring the present limit.
589	(C) To the extent that both people are worried about the consequence of drunk driving, they would probably both agree that we should be worried about someone driving at a level above the current legal limit.
590	
591	This shouldn't seem too tempting superficially, since we know the disagreement is centered on "what would happen if we lowered the blood alcohol limit", not on what's the case with the current blood alcohol limit.
592	(D) Because this is so weakly worded, it's very hard to disagree with. Miguel would be our best shot, but can we infer that he believes:

772	STIMULUS: Albert: Swenson’s popular book, which argues that sun exposure does not harm skin cells, is a model of poor scholarship. 777	(D) [1% | Unsupported Disagree Position] Swenson’s book has stimulated new research on sun exposure
778	(E) [2% | Both Positions Unsupported] something that does not stimulate new research can have value
779	--- BREAKDOWN

No, she never addresses the contents of his book. 3. His book is not valuable because it's stimulated new research on sun exposure? Yes, this is what she seems to be arguing. She's saying, "Hogwash. You're calling his poorly researched book valuable because of its consequence? Because it's stimulated new research on sun exposure. That's as crazy as saying that a virus is valuable because of its consequence of stimulating epidemiologists".

786	--- CHOICE NOTES

(Yes, Swenson believes that sun exposure doesn't harm skin cells, but we have no idea whether Albert believes that) Yvonne doesn't talk about sun exposure or skin cells at all.
788	(B) We can support that Albert agrees with this, because he explicitly says so. But can we support from Yvonne's claims that she thinks "Swenson's book is not a model of poor scholarship"? No, she doesn't talk about Swenson's book at all, so we have no idea whether she thinks it's good or bad scholarship. We only know that she thinks it's a crazy argument to say, "S's book is poor scholarship, but it's valuable because it stimulated research."
789	(C) We can support the Agree position with Albert, because he explicitly says the book is valuable. We can support the Disagree position (Swenson's book should not be considered valuable) with Yvonne. She reacts to Albert saying "The book is nonetheless valuable" by exasperatingly disagreeing: "You're kidding me!" She provides an analogy to show Albert how crazy his conclusion is. "Would you conclude that a virus is valuable because it stimulates epidemiologists? Of course not. Similarly, you shouldn't consider Swenson's book valuable just because it stimulated research."
790	(D) We can support that Albert agrees with this, because he explicitly says so. But can we support from Yvonne's claims that she thinks "Swenson's book has not stimulated new research"? No, she doesn't seem to deny that the book has stimulated new research.
791	(E) We can't infer "something that doesn't stimulate new research can have value" from either person's claims. Neither of them discuss "something that doesn't stimulate new research". They both only talk about something (Swenson's book / a new virus) that does stimulate new research. Since we have no commentary on things that don't stimulate new research, we have no support for agreeing or disagreeing with this claim. This answer is nonetheless a little tempting, because a legitimate answer could have been "something that does stimulate new research automatically has value". (Albert would agree and Yvonne would disagree with this claim)

1723	(C) Sefu clearly agrees with this, but we can't point to Annette arguing that Sefu has no vested interest in the council's votes.
1724	
1725	If anything, she seems to understand that he wants the council to vote a certain way.
1726	(D) Annette clearly agrees that other towns have successfully implemented similar plans, but we can't point to Sefu saying, "No other town has ever successfully implemented a similar plan".
1727	(E) We can plausibly infer that Sefu agrees with this. He doesn't want to do Annette's plan because it would give the appearance of undue influence, so he clearly thinks that sort of things should be avoided.
1728	
1729	But we can't point to Annette arguing that it's okay to create the appearance of undue influence. We might think to ourselves, "well if she's PUSHING for this idea of taking the council to other towns, she must think you don't need to avoid the appearance of undue influence."
1730	
1731	But it's not clear that Annette has even considered that her idea may create the appearance of undue influence.
1732

(PT145-S4-Q2, PT150-S3-Q20, PT149-S1-Q7)\n- **When no explicit claim is denied, list speaker 1's assumptions and test those.** That is how the implicit clash surfaces. (PT147-S4-Q20, PT117-S4-Q10, PT114-S2-Q19)\n- **Test every choice for both positions.** \"Can we support the Agree position? Can we support the Disagree position?\" Say which side is missing, in that speaker's words. (PT153-S2-Q9, PT145-S4-Q2)\n- **State the strength of the Disagree position.** To disagree with \"some\" or \"sometimes\" you must hold \"none\" or \"never\". That kills weakly worded traps, and the reverse kills strongly worded ones. (PT118-S3-Q20 E, PT101-S2-Q14 D and E, PT102-S4-Q22 D)\n- **Separate \"is\" from \"should\", and \"does\" from \"would\".** A speaker who only talks about what should happen gives no position on what does happen. (PT108-S3-Q20 A and B, PT149-S1-Q3 B)\n- **Watch for word-sense shifts.** Two speakers can reach \"always\" vs \"never\" by using one word two ways. (PT108-S3-Q20)\n- **Predict the disguise.** One side's position phrased in the other side's words, or an explicit clash tested at the level of the reason underneath. (PT105-S1-Q15, PT101-S2-Q14, PT138-S3-Q25)\n- **Handle conditionals.** Disagreeing with \"if A then B\" means holding that A can be true without B. A choice that reverses a speaker's conditional is unsupported. (PT138-S2-Q21, PT109-S3-Q19 E, PT140-S1-Q10 E)\n- **For Agree stems and EXCEPT stems, reset the standard out loud.** Agree usually means shared background. EXCEPT means rank how supportable each disagreement is.
